People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose it’s been creeping in before, but this time it feels… more settled in, if that makes sense. Things I used to love just sit there now.

My watercolors are still on the little table by the window, and I keep thinking I ought to rinse the brushes properly—my granddaughter gave me one of those nice sets years ago, the kind with the little metal tin—but I don’t pick them up. And the tomato plants… well, they’re there, poor things. I used to fuss over them like they were children.

Even line dancing, which I used to enjoy, and watching the cardinals out back—I used to know the pair that came every morning—now I just look and can’t make myself care much. It’s not like me.

It’s… well, I’ve had spells where I wasn’t quite myself, I suppose, but what I notice now is that the things I used to love just don’t pull me in anymore.

I used to fuss over them like they were children.\n\nEven line dancing, which I used to enjoy, and watching the cardinals out back—I used to know the pair that came every morning—now I just look and can’t make myself care much. It’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period before when you felt this down or lost interest in things for a while, or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: It’s… well, I’ve had spells where I wasn’t quite myself, I suppose, but what I notice now is that the things I used to love just don’t pull me in anymore.\n\nMy watercolors are sitting there, and the tomato plants too — I keep meaning to tend to them, you know, tomatoes need fussing or they get all leggy — and line dancing used to be such a nice thing, everyone laughing and missing steps. I’ll mix up appointment times, or I’ll write the wrong date for an exhibit—there’s one I like to follow, with quilts and little local things—and then I can’t remember which volunteer I already called. It’s all simple stuff, but lately it just slips around on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or lacking energy lately, even when you haven’t done anything physically or mentally exhausting?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s odd because I’ll sleep, and I think, well, surely I’ll feel better in the morning, but I wake up tired anyway. Like my body didn’t get the message.\n\nBy the time I wash up and make myself some breakfast—nothing fancy, just toast or cereal, though I used to like slicing peaches if they were good that week—I feel like I need to sit down again. And I think, for heaven’s sake, I haven’t even done anything yet.\n\nDoctor: Has it become harder for you to think clearly or concentrate, even on simple tasks, like reading a label or following a recipe?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s really why I came in, I suppose. My head just feels foggy. Not exactly like I don’t know things, but they won’t line up right.\n\nI’ll mix up appointment times, or I’ll put down the wrong date for an exhibit — I like keeping track of those little local exhibits, the quilts and old photographs and such — and then I can’t remember which volunteer I already called. It’s embarrassing, because these are simple things. I used to keep them straight without even thinking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like not feeling hungry or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, actually. Food just doesn’t appeal much most days. I’ll make toast, or maybe heat up some soup, and then I sort of pick at it like a bird. I used to enjoy a proper lunch, especially if there were good tomatoes—my neighbor used to grow the sweetest ones, though I don’t think she does anymore.\n\nBut yes, my skirts have gotten loose at the waist, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight. It’s not on purpose. I just don’t feel hungry.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before, where you felt down and lost interest in things, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s been creeping in before, but this time it feels… more settled in, if that makes sense. Things I used to love just sit there now.\n\nMy watercolors are still on the little table by the window, and I keep thinking I ought to rinse the brushes properly—my granddaughter gave me one of those nice sets years ago, the kind with the little metal tin—but I don’t pick them up. And the tomato plants… well, they’re there, poor things. I used to fuss over them like they were children.\n\nEven line dancing, which I used to enjoy, and watching the cardinals out back—I used to know the pair that came every morning—now I just look and can’t make myself care much. It’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period before when you felt this down or lost interest in things for a while, or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: It’s… well, I’ve had spells where I wasn’t quite myself, I suppose, but what I notice now is that the things I used to love just don’t pull me in anymore.\n\nMy watercolors are sitting there, and the tomato plants too — I keep meaning to tend to them, you know, tomatoes need fussing or they get all leggy — and line dancing used to be such a nice thing, everyone laughing and missing steps. Even the cardinals out back… I used to watch for them every morning. Now they’re just there, and I can’t seem to make myself care the way I did.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, before this current time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, dear. Two weeks… that’s hard to pin down. I know I’ve had times where I felt low or didn’t feel like doing much, but I don’t keep track of it neatly like that.\n\nMy sister used to write everything on a wall calendar—appointments, birthdays, even when to turn the mattress—but I never was that organized. Not exactly like I don’t know things, but they won’t line up right.\n\nI’ll mix up appointment times, or I’ll put down the wrong date for an exhibit — I like keeping track of those little local exhibits, the quilts and old photographs and such — and then I can’t remember which volunteer I already called. It’s embarrassing, because these are simple things. I used to keep them straight without even thinking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like not feeling hungry or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, actually. Food just doesn’t appeal much most days. I’ll make toast, or maybe heat up some soup, and then I sort of pick at it like a bird. I used to enjoy a proper lunch, especially if there were good tomatoes—my neighbor used to grow the sweetest ones, though I don’t think she does anymore.\n\nBut yes, my skirts have gotten loose at the waist, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight. It’s not on purpose. I just don’t feel hungry.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before, where you felt down and lost interest in things, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s been creeping in before, but this time it feels… more settled in, if that makes sense. Things I used to love just sit there now.\n\nMy watercolors are still on the little table by the window, and I keep thinking I ought to rinse the brushes properly—my granddaughter gave me one of those nice sets years ago, the kind with the little metal tin—but I don’t pick them up. And the tomato plants… well, they’re there, poor things. I used to fuss over them like they were children.\n\nEven line dancing, which I used to enjoy, and watching the cardinals out back—I used to know the pair that came every morning—now I just look and can’t make myself care much. It’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period before when you felt this down or lost interest in things for a while, or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: It’s… well, I’ve had spells where I wasn’t quite myself, I suppose, but what I notice now is that the things I used to love just don’t pull me in anymore.\n\nMy watercolors are sitting there, and the tomato plants too — I keep meaning to tend to them, you know, tomatoes need fussing or they get all leggy — and line dancing used to be such a nice thing, everyone laughing and missing steps. Even the cardinals out back… I used to watch for them every morning. Now they’re just there, and I can’t seem to make myself care the way I did.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, before this current time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, dear. I know I’ve had times where I felt low or didn’t feel like doing much, but I don’t keep track of it neatly like that.\n\nMy sister used to write everything on a wall calendar—appointments, birthdays, even when to turn the mattress—but I never was that organized. I don’t know about several times, exactly… I keep thinking of now, because it’s right in front of me.

My watercolors are sitting by the window, and I used to lose whole afternoons with those little pans of paint. And the tomato plants—oh, they’ll punish you if you ignore them, you know, they get all wild and leggy. Line dancing too, that used to brighten me up, seeing everyone shuffle the wrong way. Now it’s like all those things are just sitting there, and I can’t make myself care. Whether that’s happened lots of times before, I’m not sure I can say right.

And I think, for heaven’s sake, I haven’t even done anything yet.\n\nDoctor: Has it become harder for you to think clearly or concentrate, even on simple tasks, like reading a label or following a recipe?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s really why I came in, I suppose. My head just feels foggy. Not exactly like I don’t know things, but they won’t line up right.\n\nI’ll mix up appointment times, or I’ll put down the wrong date for an exhibit — I like keeping track of those little local exhibits, the quilts and old photographs and such — and then I can’t remember which volunteer I already called. It’s embarrassing, because these are simple things. I used to keep them straight without even thinking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like not feeling hungry or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, actually. Food just doesn’t appeal much most days. I’ll make toast, or maybe heat up some soup, and then I sort of pick at it like a bird. I used to enjoy a proper lunch, especially if there were good tomatoes—my neighbor used to grow the sweetest ones, though I don’t think she does anymore.\n\nBut yes, my skirts have gotten loose at the waist, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight. It’s not on purpose. I just don’t feel hungry.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before, where you felt down and lost interest in things, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s been creeping in before, but this time it feels… more settled in, if that makes sense. Things I used to love just sit there now.\n\nMy watercolors are still on the little table by the window, and I keep thinking I ought to rinse the brushes properly—my granddaughter gave me one of those nice sets years ago, the kind with the little metal tin—but I don’t pick them up. And the tomato plants… well, they’re there, poor things. I used to fuss over them like they were children.\n\nEven line dancing, which I used to enjoy, and watching the cardinals out back—I used to know the pair that came every morning—now I just look and can’t make myself care much. It’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period before when you felt this down or lost interest in things for a while, or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: It’s… well, I’ve had spells where I wasn’t quite myself, I suppose, but what I notice now is that the things I used to love just don’t pull me in anymore.\n\nMy watercolors are sitting there, and the tomato plants too — I keep meaning to tend to them, you know, tomatoes need fussing or they get all leggy — and line dancing used to be such a nice thing, everyone laughing and missing steps. Even the cardinals out back… I used to watch for them every morning. Now they’re just there, and I can’t seem to make myself care the way I did.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, before this current time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, dear. Two weeks… that’s hard to pin down. I know I’ve had times where I felt low or didn’t feel like doing much, but I don’t keep track of it neatly like that.\n\nMy sister used to write everything on a wall calendar—appointments, birthdays, even when to turn the mattress—but I never was that organized. So… before now, maybe there were spells, but I couldn’t honestly say whether it was two weeks or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time before this when you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say it neatly like “two weeks or more.” I’m sorry, I keep getting stuck on the counting part.\n\nBut this time, yes, it’s been long enough that it feels like it’s settled over everything. My watercolors are right there by the window, and the tomato plants need tending, and I used to love line dancing—oh, we’d laugh so much when someone went the wrong way—and even the cardinals out back don’t pull me in like they used to. They’re all still there, the same things I used to love, and I just… can’t make myself care.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several times in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for a while, not just once or twice but multiple times?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about several times, exactly… I keep thinking of now, because it’s right in front of me.\n\nMy watercolors are sitting by the window, and I used to lose whole afternoons with those little pans of paint. And the tomato plants—oh, they’ll punish you if you ignore them, you know, they get all wild and leggy. Line dancing too, that used to brighten me up, seeing everyone shuffle the wrong way. And the cardinals out back… I used to wait for them.\n\nNow it’s like all those things are just sitting there, and I can’t make myself care. Whether that’s happened lots of times before, I’m not sure I can say right.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m sorry, I still don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Several different times” and “more than a couple weeks”… it all gets blurry when I try to look back.

I remember being down here and there, sure, but whether it was months apart, or how long it lasted… I just didn’t mark it down. My neighbor used to keep a diary every night in those little books with flowers on the cover. I always thought I should do that, but then I’d forget after two days.

So I can’t really say yes or no. I just know this time has felt heavy, but looking back over all the years, I’m not sure.

It’s embarrassing, because these are things I used to keep straight without even thinking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have. It’s more like I just can’t seem to care about things I used to love. My watercolors are sitting right there, and I used to fuss over the colors for hours. The tomato plants too—I always liked checking them, even just seeing if the cardinals were out back near the fence—but lately I just look at it all and… nothing. Even line dancing, which used to be such a bright spot for me, I can’t make myself want to go.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things lately, even when it’s not really your fault?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I’ll fuss at myself for forgetting something, like where I put a note or whether I told someone the right time, but guilt? I’m not sure I’d call it that. \n\nMy sister used to say I worried over things like a hen over one chick—funny, she had this little ceramic hen on her windowsill—but no, I don’t think I’ve been blaming myself for things that aren’t mine. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or thinking clearly lately, like it’s harder to focus or make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s really the main thing, I think. My head feels foggy, like I’m trying to look through a dirty window. I’ll mix up appointment times, or I’ll write the wrong date for an exhibit—there’s one I like to follow, with quilts and little local things—and then I can’t remember which volunteer I already called. It’s all simple stuff, but lately it just slips around on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or lacking energy lately, even when you haven’t done anything physically or mentally exhausting?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s odd because I’ll sleep, and I think, well, surely I’ll feel better in the morning, but I wake up tired anyway. Like my body didn’t get the message.\n\nBy the time I wash up and make myself some breakfast—nothing fancy, just toast or cereal, though I used to like slicing peaches if they were good that week—I feel like I need to sit down again. And I think, for heaven’s sake, I haven’t even done anything yet.\n\nDoctor: Has it become harder for you to think clearly or concentrate, even on simple tasks, like reading a label or following a recipe?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s really why I came in, I suppose. My head just feels foggy. Not exactly like I don’t know things, but they won’t line up right.\n\nI’ll mix up appointment times, or I’ll put down the wrong date for an exhibit — I like keeping track of those little local exhibits, the quilts and old photographs and such — and then I can’t remember which volunteer I already called. It’s embarrassing, because these are simple things. I used to keep them straight without even thinking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like not feeling hungry or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, actually. Food just doesn’t appeal much most days. I’ll make toast, or maybe heat up some soup, and then I sort of pick at it like a bird. I used to enjoy a proper lunch, especially if there were good tomatoes—my neighbor used to grow the sweetest ones, though I don’t think she does anymore.\n\nBut yes, my skirts have gotten loose at the waist, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight. It’s not on purpose. I just don’t feel hungry.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before, where you felt down and lost interest in things, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s been creeping in before, but this time it feels… more settled in, if that makes sense. Things I used to love just sit there now.\n\nMy watercolors are still on the little table by the window, and I keep thinking I ought to rinse the brushes properly—my granddaughter gave me one of those nice sets years ago, the kind with the little metal tin—but I don’t pick them up. And the tomato plants… well, they’re there, poor things. I used to fuss over them like they were children.\n\nEven line dancing, which I used to enjoy, and watching the cardinals out back—I used to know the pair that came every morning—now I just look and can’t make myself care much. It’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period before when you felt this down or lost interest in things for a while, or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: It’s… well, I’ve had spells where I wasn’t quite myself, I suppose, but what I notice now is that the things I used to love just don’t pull me in anymore.\n\nMy watercolors are sitting there, and the tomato plants too — I keep meaning to tend to them, you know, tomatoes need fussing or they get all leggy — and line dancing used to be such a nice thing, everyone laughing and missing steps. Even the cardinals out back… I used to watch for them every morning. Now they’re just there, and I can’t seem to make myself care the way I did.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, before this current time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, dear. Two weeks… that’s hard to pin down. I know I’ve had times where I felt low or didn’t feel like doing much, but I don’t keep track of it neatly like that.\n\nMy sister used to write everything on a wall calendar—appointments, birthdays, even when to turn the mattress—but I never was that organized. So… before now, maybe there were spells, but I couldn’t honestly say whether it was two weeks or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time before this when you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say it neatly like “two weeks or more.” I’m sorry, I keep getting stuck on the counting part.\n\nBut this time, yes, it’s been long enough that it feels like it’s settled over everything. My watercolors are right there by the window, and the tomato plants need tending, and I used to love line dancing—oh, we’d laugh so much when someone went the wrong way—and even the cardinals out back don’t pull me in like they used to. They’re all still there, the same things I used to love, and I just… can’t make myself care.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several times in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for a while, not just once or twice but multiple times?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about several times, exactly… I keep thinking of now, because it’s right in front of me.\n\nMy watercolors are sitting by the window, and I used to lose whole afternoons with those little pans of paint. And the tomato plants—oh, they’ll punish you if you ignore them, you know, they get all wild and leggy. Line dancing too, that used to brighten me up, seeing everyone shuffle the wrong way. And the cardinals out back… I used to wait for them.\n\nNow it’s like all those things are just sitting there, and I can’t make myself care. Whether that’s happened lots of times before, I’m not sure I can say right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, maybe separated by months or years, when you felt down or lost interest in things for more than a couple of weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Several different times” and “more than a couple weeks”… it all gets blurry when I try to look back.\n\nI remember being down here and there, sure, but whether it was months apart, or how long it lasted… I just didn’t mark it down. My neighbor used to keep a diary every night in those little books with flowers on the cover. I always thought I should do that, but then I’d forget after two days.\n\nSo I can’t really say yes or no. I just know this time has felt heavy, but looking back over all the years, I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "candidates": ["F32.9", "F43.22"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

I just know this time has felt heavy, but looking back over all the years, I’m not sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had several different times in your life, separated by months or even years, when you felt down or lost interest in things for more than a couple of weeks at a time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 79 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know you’re asking me the same thing, and I’m trying, I really am. I just… I don’t have a neat answer for that.

When I look back, it’s like looking through an old photo box where nothing is in order. Some years I remember by who was visiting, or what was blooming in the yard, not by whether I felt down for two weeks or three weeks. I can remember times I wasn’t myself, but I can’t honestly say they were separate like that, months or years apart, and lasted that long.

So… I’m not trying to dodge you. I just don’t know for sure.

It’s more like I just can’t seem to care about things I used to love. My watercolors are sitting right there, and I used to fuss over the colors for hours. The tomato plants too—I always liked checking them, even just seeing if the cardinals were out back near the fence—but lately I just look at it all and… nothing. Even line dancing, which used to be such a bright spot for me, I can’t make myself want to go.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things lately, even when it’s not really your fault?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not especially, I don’t think. I’m not sure I’d call it that. \n\nMy sister used to say I worried over things like a hen over one chick—funny, she had this little ceramic hen on her windowsill—but no, I don’t think I’ve been blaming myself for things that aren’t mine. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or thinking clearly lately, like it’s harder to focus or make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s really the main thing, I think. My head feels foggy, like I’m trying to look through a dirty window. I’ll mix up appointment times, or I’ll write the wrong date for an exhibit—there’s one I like to follow, with quilts and little local things—and then I can’t remember which volunteer I already called. It’s all simple stuff, but lately it just slips around on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or lacking energy lately, even when you haven’t done anything physically or mentally exhausting?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s odd because I’ll sleep, and I think, well, surely I’ll feel better in the morning, but I wake up tired anyway. Like my body didn’t get the message.\n\nBy the time I wash up and make myself some breakfast—nothing fancy, just toast or cereal, though I used to like slicing peaches if they were good that week—I feel like I need to sit down again. And I think, for heaven’s sake, I haven’t even done anything yet.\n\nDoctor: Has it become harder for you to think clearly or concentrate, even on simple tasks, like reading a label or following a recipe?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s really why I came in, I suppose. My head just feels foggy. Not exactly like I don’t know things, but they won’t line up right.\n\nI’ll mix up appointment times, or I’ll put down the wrong date for an exhibit — I like keeping track of those little local exhibits, the quilts and old photographs and such — and then I can’t remember which volunteer I already called. It’s embarrassing, because these are simple things. I used to keep them straight without even thinking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like not feeling hungry or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, actually. Food just doesn’t appeal much most days. I’ll make toast, or maybe heat up some soup, and then I sort of pick at it like a bird. I used to enjoy a proper lunch, especially if there were good tomatoes—my neighbor used to grow the sweetest ones, though I don’t think she does anymore.\n\nBut yes, my skirts have gotten loose at the waist, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight. It’s not on purpose. I just don’t feel hungry.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before, where you felt down and lost interest in things, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s been creeping in before, but this time it feels… more settled in, if that makes sense. Things I used to love just sit there now.\n\nMy watercolors are still on the little table by the window, and I keep thinking I ought to rinse the brushes properly—my granddaughter gave me one of those nice sets years ago, the kind with the little metal tin—but I don’t pick them up. And the tomato plants… well, they’re there, poor things. I used to fuss over them like they were children.\n\nEven line dancing, which I used to enjoy, and watching the cardinals out back—I used to know the pair that came every morning—now I just look and can’t make myself care much. It’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period before when you felt this down or lost interest in things for a while, or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: It’s… well, I’ve had spells where I wasn’t quite myself, I suppose, but what I notice now is that the things I used to love just don’t pull me in anymore.\n\nMy watercolors are sitting there, and the tomato plants too — I keep meaning to tend to them, you know, tomatoes need fussing or they get all leggy — and line dancing used to be such a nice thing, everyone laughing and missing steps. Even the cardinals out back… I used to watch for them every morning. Now they’re just there, and I can’t seem to make myself care the way I did.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, before this current time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, dear. Two weeks… that’s hard to pin down. I know I’ve had times where I felt low or didn’t feel like doing much, but I don’t keep track of it neatly like that.\n\nMy sister used to write everything on a wall calendar—appointments, birthdays, even when to turn the mattress—but I never was that organized. So… before now, maybe there were spells, but I couldn’t honestly say whether it was two weeks or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time before this when you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say it neatly like “two weeks or more.” I’m sorry, I keep getting stuck on the counting part.\n\nBut this time, yes, it’s been long enough that it feels like it’s settled over everything. And the tomato plants—oh, they’ll punish you if you ignore them, you know, they get all wild and leggy. Line dancing too, that used to brighten me up, seeing everyone shuffle the wrong way. And the cardinals out back… I used to wait for them.\n\nNow it’s like all those things are just sitting there, and I can’t make myself care. Whether that’s happened lots of times before, I’m not sure I can say right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, maybe separated by months or years, when you felt down or lost interest in things for more than a couple of weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Several different times” and “more than a couple weeks”… it all gets blurry when I try to look back.\n\nI remember being down here and there, sure, but whether it was months apart, or how long it lasted… I just didn’t mark it down. I just know this time has felt heavy, but looking back over all the years, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, separated by months or even years, when you felt down or lost interest in things for more than a couple of weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking me the same thing, and I’m trying, I really am. My neighbor used to keep a diary every night in those little books with flowers on the cover. I always thought I should do that, but then I’d forget after two days.\n\nSo I can’t really say yes or no. I just know this time has felt heavy, but looking back over all the years, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, separated by months or even years, when you felt down or lost interest in things for more than a couple of weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking me the same thing, and I’m trying, I really am. I just… I don’t have a neat answer for that.\n\nWhen I look back, it’s like looking through an old photo box where nothing is in order. Some years I remember by who was visiting, or what was blooming in the yard, not by whether I felt down for two weeks or three weeks. I can remember times I wasn’t myself, but I can’t honestly say they were separate like that, months or years apart, and lasted that long.\n\nSo… I’m not trying to dodge you. I just don’t know for sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had several different times in your life, each lasting more than two weeks, when you felt down or lost interest in things, and those times were separated by at least a couple of months or more?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 15\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]

{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The doctor is asking about a history of multiple separate depressive episodes over the lifetime, each lasting more than two weeks and separated by months. The provided symptom profile covers current loss of interest and current depressive duration, but not recurrence of multiple past episodes."}

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wish I could give you a better answer, but I’m still not sure. I don’t keep time in my head that way—two weeks, two months, separated by this or that.

I can remember spells where I felt low or didn’t care about things, but when you ask whether there were several of them, each longer than two weeks, and separated out like that… it all runs together. Like when you open a drawer full of old greeting cards and none of them have the envelopes anymore, so you can’t tell what year they were from.

So I can’t honestly say yes. I can’t honestly say no either. I just don’t know.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brought me in is… my head just feels foggy lately. I keep mixing up simple things, like appointment times, and then I’ll write down an exhibit date wrong—there’s a little local exhibit I like to keep track of, you know, the kind with quilts and old photographs—and then I can’t remember which volunteer I already called. It’s embarrassing, because these are things I used to keep straight without even thinking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have. It’s more like I just can’t seem to care about things I used to love. My watercolors are sitting right there, and I used to fuss over the colors for hours. The tomato plants too—I always liked checking them, even just seeing if the cardinals were out back near the fence—but lately I just look at it all and… nothing. Even line dancing, which used to be such a bright spot for me, I can’t make myself want to go.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things lately, even when it’s not really your fault?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I’ll fuss at myself for forgetting something, like where I put a note or whether I told someone the right time, but guilt? I’m not sure I’d call it that. \n\nMy sister used to say I worried over things like a hen over one chick—funny, she had this little ceramic hen on her windowsill—but no, I don’t think I’ve been blaming myself for things that aren’t mine. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or thinking clearly lately, like it’s harder to focus or make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s really the main thing, I think. My head feels foggy, like I’m trying to look through a dirty window. I’ll mix up appointment times, or I’ll write the wrong date for an exhibit—there’s one I like to follow, with quilts and little local things—and then I can’t remember which volunteer I already called. It’s all simple stuff, but lately it just slips around on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or lacking energy lately, even when you haven’t done anything physically or mentally exhausting?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s odd because I’ll sleep, and I think, well, surely I’ll feel better in the morning, but I wake up tired anyway. Like my body didn’t get the message.\n\nBy the time I wash up and make myself some breakfast—nothing fancy, just toast or cereal, though I used to like slicing peaches if they were good that week—I feel like I need to sit down again. And I think, for heaven’s sake, I haven’t even done anything yet.\n\nDoctor: Has it become harder for you to think clearly or concentrate, even on simple tasks, like reading a label or following a recipe?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s really why I came in, I suppose. My head just feels foggy. Not exactly like I don’t know things, but they won’t line up right.\n\nI’ll mix up appointment times, or I’ll put down the wrong date for an exhibit — I like keeping track of those little local exhibits, the quilts and old photographs and such — and then I can’t remember which volunteer I already called. It’s embarrassing, because these are simple things. I used to keep them straight without even thinking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like not feeling hungry or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, actually. Food just doesn’t appeal much most days. I’ll make toast, or maybe heat up some soup, and then I sort of pick at it like a bird. I used to enjoy a proper lunch, especially if there were good tomatoes—my neighbor used to grow the sweetest ones, though I don’t think she does anymore.\n\nBut yes, my skirts have gotten loose at the waist, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight. It’s not on purpose. I just don’t feel hungry.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before, where you felt down and lost interest in things, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s been creeping in before, but this time it feels… more settled in, if that makes sense. Things I used to love just sit there now.\n\nMy watercolors are still on the little table by the window, and I keep thinking I ought to rinse the brushes properly—my granddaughter gave me one of those nice sets years ago, the kind with the little metal tin—but I don’t pick them up. And the tomato plants… well, they’re there, poor things. I used to fuss over them like they were children.\n\nEven line dancing, which I used to enjoy, and watching the cardinals out back—I used to know the pair that came every morning—now I just look and can’t make myself care much. It’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period before when you felt this down or lost interest in things for a while, or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: It’s… well, I’ve had spells where I wasn’t quite myself, I suppose, but what I notice now is that the things I used to love just don’t pull me in anymore.\n\nMy watercolors are sitting there, and the tomato plants too — I keep meaning to tend to them, you know, tomatoes need fussing or they get all leggy — and line dancing used to be such a nice thing, everyone laughing and missing steps. Even the cardinals out back… I used to watch for them every morning. Now they’re just there, and I can’t seem to make myself care the way I did.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, before this current time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, dear. Two weeks… that’s hard to pin down. I know I’ve had times where I felt low or didn’t feel like doing much, but I don’t keep track of it neatly like that.\n\nMy sister used to write everything on a wall calendar—appointments, birthdays, even when to turn the mattress—but I never was that organized. So… before now, maybe there were spells, but I couldn’t honestly say whether it was two weeks or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time before this when you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say it neatly like “two weeks or more.” I’m sorry, I keep getting stuck on the counting part.\n\nBut this time, yes, it’s been long enough that it feels like it’s settled over everything. My watercolors are right there by the window, and the tomato plants need tending, and I used to love line dancing—oh, we’d laugh so much when someone went the wrong way—and even the cardinals out back don’t pull me in like they used to. They’re all still there, the same things I used to love, and I just… can’t make myself care.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several times in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for a while, not just once or twice but multiple times?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about several times, exactly… I keep thinking of now, because it’s right in front of me.\n\nMy watercolors are sitting by the window, and I used to lose whole afternoons with those little pans of paint. And the tomato plants—oh, they’ll punish you if you ignore them, you know, they get all wild and leggy. Line dancing too, that used to brighten me up, seeing everyone shuffle the wrong way. And the cardinals out back… I used to wait for them.\n\nNow it’s like all those things are just sitting there, and I can’t make myself care. Whether that’s happened lots of times before, I’m not sure I can say right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, maybe separated by months or years, when you felt down or lost interest in things for more than a couple of weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Several different times” and “more than a couple weeks”… it all gets blurry when I try to look back.\n\nI remember being down here and there, sure, but whether it was months apart, or how long it lasted… I just didn’t mark it down. My neighbor used to keep a diary every night in those little books with flowers on the cover. I always thought I should do that, but then I’d forget after two days.\n\nSo I can’t really say yes or no. I just know this time has felt heavy, but looking back over all the years, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, separated by months or even years, when you felt down or lost interest in things for more than a couple of weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking me the same thing, and I’m trying, I really am. I just… I don’t have a neat answer for that.\n\nWhen I look back, it’s like looking through an old photo box where nothing is in order. Some years I remember by who was visiting, or what was blooming in the yard, not by whether I felt down for two weeks or three weeks. I can remember times I wasn’t myself, but I can’t honestly say they were separate like that, months or years apart, and lasted that long.\n\nSo… I’m not trying to dodge you. I just don’t know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, each lasting more than two weeks, when you felt down or lost interest in things, and those times were separated by at least a couple of months or more?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a better answer, but I’m still not sure. I don’t keep time in my head that way—two weeks, two months, separated by this or that.\n\nI can remember spells where I felt low or didn’t care about things, but when you ask whether there were several of them, each longer than two weeks, and separated out like that… it all runs together. Like when you open a drawer full of old greeting cards and none of them have the envelopes anymore, so you can’t tell what year they were from.\n\nSo I can’t honestly say yes. I can’t honestly say no either. I just don’t know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I hear what you’re asking, doctor, I do. I just can’t sort it out that neatly in my head.

I know there were times I felt down or sort of lost my spark, but whether there were several separate times, and each one was more than two weeks, with months in between… I can’t honestly pin that down. Time gets funny when I look back that far.

I mean, I’ll fuss at myself for forgetting something, like where I put a note or whether I told someone the right time, but guilt? I’m not sure I’d call it that. \n\nMy sister used to say I worried over things like a hen over one chick—funny, she had this little ceramic hen on her windowsill—but no, I don’t think I’ve been blaming myself for things that aren’t mine. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or thinking clearly lately, like it’s harder to focus or make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s really the main thing, I think. My head feels foggy, like I’m trying to look through a dirty window. I’ll mix up appointment times, or I’ll write the wrong date for an exhibit—there’s one I like to follow, with quilts and little local things—and then I can’t remember which volunteer I already called. It’s all simple stuff, but lately it just slips around on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or lacking energy lately, even when you haven’t done anything physically or mentally exhausting?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s odd because I’ll sleep, and I think, well, surely I’ll feel better in the morning, but I wake up tired anyway. Like my body didn’t get the message.\n\nBy the time I wash up and make myself some breakfast—nothing fancy, just toast or cereal, though I used to like slicing peaches if they were good that week—I feel like I need to sit down again. And I think, for heaven’s sake, I haven’t even done anything yet.\n\nDoctor: Has it become harder for you to think clearly or concentrate, even on simple tasks, like reading a label or following a recipe?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s really why I came in, I suppose. My head just feels foggy. Not exactly like I don’t know things, but they won’t line up right.\n\nI’ll mix up appointment times, or I’ll put down the wrong date for an exhibit — I like keeping track of those little local exhibits, the quilts and old photographs and such — and then I can’t remember which volunteer I already called. It’s embarrassing, because these are simple things. I used to keep them straight without even thinking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like not feeling hungry or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, actually. Food just doesn’t appeal much most days. I’ll make toast, or maybe heat up some soup, and then I sort of pick at it like a bird. I used to enjoy a proper lunch, especially if there were good tomatoes—my neighbor used to grow the sweetest ones, though I don’t think she does anymore.\n\nBut yes, my skirts have gotten loose at the waist, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight. It’s not on purpose. I just don’t feel hungry.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before, where you felt down and lost interest in things, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s been creeping in before, but this time it feels… more settled in, if that makes sense. Things I used to love just sit there now.\n\nMy watercolors are still on the little table by the window, and I keep thinking I ought to rinse the brushes properly—my granddaughter gave me one of those nice sets years ago, the kind with the little metal tin—but I don’t pick them up. And the tomato plants… well, they’re there, poor things. I used to fuss over them like they were children.\n\nEven line dancing, which I used to enjoy, and watching the cardinals out back—I used to know the pair that came every morning—now I just look and can’t make myself care much. It’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period before when you felt this down or lost interest in things for a while, or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: It’s… well, I’ve had spells where I wasn’t quite myself, I suppose, but what I notice now is that the things I used to love just don’t pull me in anymore.\n\nMy watercolors are sitting there, and the tomato plants too — I keep meaning to tend to them, you know, tomatoes need fussing or they get all leggy — and line dancing used to be such a nice thing, everyone laughing and missing steps. Even the cardinals out back… I used to watch for them every morning. Now they’re just there, and I can’t seem to make myself care the way I did.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, before this current time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, dear. Two weeks… that’s hard to pin down. I know I’ve had times where I felt low or didn’t feel like doing much, but I don’t keep track of it neatly like that.\n\nMy sister used to write everything on a wall calendar—appointments, birthdays, even when to turn the mattress—but I never was that organized. So… before now, maybe there were spells, but I couldn’t honestly say whether it was two weeks or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time before this when you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say it neatly like “two weeks or more.” I’m sorry, I keep getting stuck on the counting part.\n\nBut this time, yes, it’s been long enough that it feels like it’s settled over everything. My watercolors are right there by the window, and the tomato plants need tending, and I used to love line dancing—oh, we’d laugh so much when someone went the wrong way—and even the cardinals out back don’t pull me in like they used to. They’re all still there, the same things I used to love, and I just… can’t make myself care.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several times in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for a while, not just once or twice but multiple times?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about several times, exactly… I keep thinking of now, because it’s right in front of me.\n\nMy watercolors are sitting by the window, and I used to lose whole afternoons with those little pans of paint. And the tomato plants—oh, they’ll punish you if you ignore them, you know, they get all wild and leggy. Line dancing too, that used to brighten me up, seeing everyone shuffle the wrong way. And the cardinals out back… I used to wait for them.\n\nNow it’s like all those things are just sitting there, and I can’t make myself care. Whether that’s happened lots of times before, I’m not sure I can say right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, maybe separated by months or years, when you felt down or lost interest in things for more than a couple of weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Several different times” and “more than a couple weeks”… it all gets blurry when I try to look back.\n\nI remember being down here and there, sure, but whether it was months apart, or how long it lasted… I just didn’t mark it down. My neighbor used to keep a diary every night in those little books with flowers on the cover. I always thought I should do that, but then I’d forget after two days.\n\nSo I can’t really say yes or no. I just know this time has felt heavy, but looking back over all the years, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, separated by months or even years, when you felt down or lost interest in things for more than a couple of weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking me the same thing, and I’m trying, I really am. I just… I don’t have a neat answer for that.\n\nWhen I look back, it’s like looking through an old photo box where nothing is in order. Some years I remember by who was visiting, or what was blooming in the yard, not by whether I felt down for two weeks or three weeks. I can remember times I wasn’t myself, but I can’t honestly say they were separate like that, months or years apart, and lasted that long.\n\nSo… I’m not trying to dodge you. I just don’t know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, each lasting more than two weeks, when you felt down or lost interest in things, and those times were separated by at least a couple of months or more?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a better answer, but I’m still not sure. I don’t keep time in my head that way—two weeks, two months, separated by this or that.\n\nI can remember spells where I felt low or didn’t care about things, but when you ask whether there were several of them, each longer than two weeks, and separated out like that… it all runs together. Like when you open a drawer full of old greeting cards and none of them have the envelopes anymore, so you can’t tell what year they were from.\n\nSo I can’t honestly say yes. I can’t honestly say no either. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, each lasting more than two weeks, when you felt down or lost interest in things, and those times were separated by at least a couple of months or more?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, doctor, I do. I just can’t sort it out that neatly in my head.\n\nI know there were times I felt down or sort of lost my spark, but whether there were several separate times, and each one was more than two weeks, with months in between… I can’t honestly pin that down. Time gets funny when I look back that far.\n\nIt reminds me of trying to remember when we painted the kitchen—my sister says it was one year, I’m sure it was another, and then we start talking about who brought the casserole and forget the paint altogether. That’s kind of how my memory is with this.\n\nSo I’m sorry, I don’t know for sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Things I used to love just sit there now.\n\nMy watercolors are still on the little table by the window, and I keep thinking I ought to rinse the brushes properly—my granddaughter gave me one of those nice sets years ago, the kind with the little metal tin—but I don’t pick them up. And the tomato plants… well, they’re there, poor things. I used to fuss over them like they were children.\n\nEven line dancing, which I used to enjoy, and watching the cardinals out back—I used to know the pair that came every morning—now I just look and can’t make myself care much. It’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period before when you felt this down or lost interest in things for a while, or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: It’s… well, I’ve had spells where I wasn’t quite myself, I suppose, but what I notice now is that the things I used to love just don’t pull me in anymore.\n\nMy watercolors are sitting there, and the tomato plants too — I keep meaning to tend to them, you know, tomatoes need fussing or they get all leggy — and line dancing used to be such a nice thing, everyone laughing and missing steps. Even the cardinals out back… I used to watch for them every morning. Now they’re just there, and I can’t seem to make myself care the way I did.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, before this current time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, dear. Two weeks… that’s hard to pin down. I know I’ve had times where I felt low or didn’t feel like doing much, but I don’t keep track of it neatly like that.\n\nMy sister used to write everything on a wall calendar—appointments, birthdays, even when to turn the mattress—but I never was that organized. So… before now, maybe there were spells, but I couldn’t honestly say whether it was two weeks or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time before this when you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say it neatly like “two weeks or more.” I’m sorry, I keep getting stuck on the counting part.\n\nBut this time, yes, it’s been long enough that it feels like it’s settled over everything. My watercolors are right there by the window, and the tomato plants need tending, and I used to love line dancing—oh, we’d laugh so much when someone went the wrong way—and even the cardinals out back don’t pull me in like they used to. They’re all still there, the same things I used to love, and I just… can’t make myself care.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several times in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for a while, not just once or twice but multiple times?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about several times, exactly… I keep thinking of now, because it’s right in front of me.\n\nMy watercolors are sitting by the window, and I used to lose whole afternoons with those little pans of paint. And the tomato plants—oh, they’ll punish you if you ignore them, you know, they get all wild and leggy. Line dancing too, that used to brighten me up, seeing everyone shuffle the wrong way. And the cardinals out back… I used to wait for them.\n\nNow it’s like all those things are just sitting there, and I can’t make myself care. Whether that’s happened lots of times before, I’m not sure I can say right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, maybe separated by months or years, when you felt down or lost interest in things for more than a couple of weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Several different times” and “more than a couple weeks”… it all gets blurry when I try to look back.\n\nI remember being down here and there, sure, but whether it was months apart, or how long it lasted… I just didn’t mark it down. My neighbor used to keep a diary every night in those little books with flowers on the cover. I always thought I should do that, but then I’d forget after two days.\n\nSo I can’t really say yes or no. I just know this time has felt heavy, but looking back over all the years, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, separated by months or even years, when you felt down or lost interest in things for more than a couple of weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking me the same thing, and I’m trying, I really am. I just… I don’t have a neat answer for that.\n\nWhen I look back, it’s like looking through an old photo box where nothing is in order. Some years I remember by who was visiting, or what was blooming in the yard, not by whether I felt down for two weeks or three weeks. I can remember times I wasn’t myself, but I can’t honestly say they were separate like that, months or years apart, and lasted that long.\n\nSo… I’m not trying to dodge you. I just don’t know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, each lasting more than two weeks, when you felt down or lost interest in things, and those times were separated by at least a couple of months or more?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a better answer, but I’m still not sure. I don’t keep time in my head that way—two weeks, two months, separated by this or that.\n\nI can remember spells where I felt low or didn’t care about things, but when you ask whether there were several of them, each longer than two weeks, and separated out like that… it all runs together. Like when you open a drawer full of old greeting cards and none of them have the envelopes anymore, so you can’t tell what year they were from.\n\nSo I can’t honestly say yes. I can’t honestly say no either. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, each lasting more than two weeks, when you felt down or lost interest in things, and those times were separated by at least a couple of months or more?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, doctor, I do. I just can’t sort it out that neatly in my head.\n\nI know there were times I felt down or sort of lost my spark, but whether there were several separate times, and each one was more than two weeks, with months in between… I can’t honestly pin that down. Time gets funny when I look back that far.\n\nIt reminds me of trying to remember when we painted the kitchen—my sister says it was one year, I’m sure it was another, and then we start talking about who brought the casserole and forget the paint altogether. That’s kind of how my memory is with this.\n\nSo I’m sorry, I don’t know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things, each lasting more than two weeks, and with at least a few months in between those times?\n\nPatient: I understand what you’re getting at, but I still can’t place it that clearly. I remember feeling down here and there, and sometimes things just didn’t interest me much, but whether it was more than two weeks each time and then months in between… I don’t know.\n\nAt my age, the years get mixed up. I’ll remember something like what curtains I had in the living room, or who came over for Sunday dinner, but not exactly how long I felt a certain way. So I can’t honestly give you a definite yes or no on that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I don’t keep time in my head that way—two weeks, two months, separated by this or that.\n\nI can remember spells where I felt low or didn’t care about things, but when you ask whether there were several of them, each longer than two weeks, and separated out like that… it all runs together. Like when you open a drawer full of old greeting cards and none of them have the envelopes anymore, so you can’t tell what year they were from.\n\nSo I can’t honestly say yes. I can’t honestly say no either. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life, each lasting more than two weeks, when you felt down or lost interest in things, and those times were separated by at least a couple of months or more?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, doctor, I do. I just can’t sort it out that neatly in my head.\n\nI know there were times I felt down or sort of lost my spark, but whether there were several separate times, and each one was more than two weeks, with months in between… I can’t honestly pin that down. Time gets funny when I look back that far.\n\nIt reminds me of trying to remember when we painted the kitchen—my sister says it was one year, I’m sure it was another, and then we start talking about who brought the casserole and forget the paint altogether. That’s kind of how my memory is with this.\n\nSo I’m sorry, I don’t know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several different times in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things, each lasting more than two weeks, and with at least a few months in between those times?\n\nPatient: I understand what you’re getting at, but I still can’t place it that clearly. I remember feeling down here and there, and sometimes things just didn’t interest me much, but whether it was more than two weeks each time and then months in between… I don’t know.\n\nAt my age, the years get mixed up. I’ll remember something like what curtains I had in the living room, or who came over for Sunday dinner, but not exactly how long I felt a certain way. So I can’t honestly give you a definite yes or no on that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had several different times in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things, each lasting more than two weeks, and with at least a few months in between those times?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 79 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m sorry, I know you’ve asked me that a few times, and I’m probably not being very helpful. I just can’t really separate it out that way.

I’ve had times when I felt low or didn’t much care about things, yes, but saying there were several separate spells, each over two weeks, with months between them… I don’t trust my memory enough to say. Sometimes I remember around holidays or what house someone was living in, not how many weeks something lasted.

So the honest answer is, I’m not sure.
